    The last-named writers have rather put the Baconian party in a corner. What they mean to say is that just as it is true that Bacon did not write these plays, so it is equally true that the boor of Stratford on the bank of the Avon never wrote them either.

[^00b-10]: [In English in the original:] "But the situation has greatly changed since Rai Shahib Dinesh Chandra Sen began to publish his patient and scientific researches into the History of Bengali Language and literature. The worth of his painstaking labours was immediately recognised in the West by such well-known Orientalists as Barth, Senart, Rhys Davids, Grierson, Barnett, Kern and Oldenberg……It is generally admitted that one of the most valuable chapters in Mr. Sens History is the lengthy one in which he treats of the Vaisnava Literature of Bengal. He has returned to his subject in his Vaisnava Literature of Medieval Bengal and in his Chaitanya and his companions, both published in 1917, while in his Banga Sahitya Parichaya (1914) will be found the Bengali texts of many Vaisnava lyrics and extracts from their historical works. Though the Rai Shahib is not himself a Vaisnava, he brings to the enterpretation of the Vaisnava Literature of his country a fine enthusiasm and a sympathetic imagination. At the same time his imagination and sympathy are controlled by his historical sense."

## A Fuller Account of Govinda Karmakar

We have learnt from the Karcha that Govinda Karmakar was born at Kanchananagar (Burdwan) in Uttara Rarh. His father's name was Shyamadasa Karmakar and his mother's Madhavi. His wife Shashimukhi one day abused him, calling him "good-for-nothing" and "fool" (p. 1). Stung by that insult, he left home in 1508 CE (Shaka 1430).

At this time Chaitanya was gaining fame as an incarnation of God. Govinda Karmakar went to Katwa, heard of this, and set out for Navadvipa.

At Navadvipa he saw Chaitanya bathing in the river surrounded by his companions. A fisherman told him who those companions were. The moment he saw the Lord, Govinda sold his heart at his lotus feet; at the very sight of Chaitanya's form he was enchanted: "A towel tied about his waist, a wondrous sight; with him an Avadhuta of serene face. ** Sitting on the ghat, I beheld this lila with my eyes. I know not what feeling arose in my mind: my body bristled like a kadamba flower; my whole body began to tremble; my body broke into sweat and my clothes were soaked. I long to wash his feet with my tears" (p. 3).

From 1508 CE, probably until Lord Chaitanya's disappearance, Govinda was his follower. When Chaitanya, resolved on taking sannyasa, set out for Katwa by way of Burdwan,  Shashimukhi once caught hold of Govinda. Though Mahaprabhu tried to dissuade Shashimukhi, he was moved by her weeping and in the end ordered Govinda to go back home. But when Chaitanya had left, Govinda disobeyed that order and, disregarding the entreaties and pleas of his relatives, followed the Lord at a run. It seems to us that, for fear of falling into Shashimukhi's clutches again and of Mahaprabhu forcing him to go back home, he kept the Karcha entirely hidden ("I keep the karcha in deep secrecy," p. 62), that is, he let no one see it.

Next, we find in the Karcha that Chaitanya, after returning to Puri, ordered Govinda to go to Shantipur with a letter. But his utterly devoted follower, thinking of a few days' separation, broke down in tears ("Hearing these words, tears flowed from my eyes; my soul cannot bear the arrow of separation from the Lord," p. 86). There was another reason for this weeping: if he went to Bengal, Shashimukhi might try to take him back; his whole life was in Mahaprabhu, and without him he was "a shadow without a body".

Here the Karcha ends. Whether the Karcha contained any further account after this cannot be said, for the manuscript may also have been incomplete.

But this much is certain: unless Govinda died suddenly after this, struck by lightning, he could not have stayed away from Mahaprabhu. A man who was distraught at the prospect of two days' separation, who disobeyed Mahaprabhu's order, fled from his wife at full speed and made his life blessed by following him like a shadow, could never have left Mahaprabhu while he lived.

What I believe to be the account of him that has since been found is given below.

I have already written that the University's manuscript library has an old manuscript called the Chaitanya-chandrodaya-kaumudi, composed by Premadasa. Although this manuscript is based mainly on Kavi Karnapura's Chaitanya-chandrodaya-nataka, it contains some extraneous matter. On p. 148 of this manuscript it is written that after Mahaprabhu's return from the South a person named Govinda Dasa arrived at Srikhanda. The manuscript also hints that this person was a shudra[^00b-11]. It appears that he was keeping everything about himself very secret. When asked  where his home was, he said: "My home is in Uttara Rarh." And Kanchananagar is indeed in Uttara Rarh. Intent on passing himself off as a complete stranger, he described himself as a "foreigner". Govinda Dasa went from Srikhanda to Shantipur, met Advaita, and then went back to Puri again with Shivananda Sena. This account is in the Chaitanya-chandrodaya-kaumudi. Premadasa refers to him as "Sri Govinda".

Now, if this incident is joined on to the point where the Karcha ends, it seems as though we had found part of the sequel to Govinda Dasa's being ordered by Mahaprabhu to go to Shantipur.

In the Chaitanya-charitamrita we see that when Shivananda Sena came to Puri, a man of the shudra caste named Govinda Dasa, introducing himself as "I am the servant of Ishvara Puri", took up the service of Mahaprabhu. Mahaprabhu had very few devotees and intimates like this servant. He is the famous "Sri Govinda" of Vaishnava history. Had Govinda Dasa revealed his identity here, Shashimukhi, on getting the news, would not have rested until she had taken him away. For nearly two years Govinda had followed Chaitanya; Shashimukhi had certainly heard that Mahaprabhu had taken him along to the South, and was waiting. In such a situation it was absolutely necessary for Govinda Dasa to conceal his identity. That without a word of introduction, just at this time, a man of the shudra caste called Govinda, suddenly announcing himself as Ishvara Puri's servant, became so intimate with Mahaprabhu is indeed very remarkable.

The Chaitanya-charitamrita, following the Chaitanya-chandrodaya, writes that although so many brahmins were eager to serve him, he showed favour to the shudra servant merely on hearing Ishvara Puri's name. In fact we are already familiar with this favour. We have been able to learn that Govinda, ordered by Chaitanya to go to Shantipur, went there and came back to Puri with Shivananda Sena's party. Having come to Puri, he certainly could never have lived apart from Mahaprabhu, his life, his soul, the object of his meditation and knowledge; and when we see that just at that time a Govinda Dasa (of the shudra caste), introducing himself as Ishvara Puri's servant, set about serving the Lord, we readily form the impression that Mahaprabhu had no other servant so intimate besides Govinda Dasa of Kanchananagar, and that the two Govindas are one and the same person, who had to cover himself up in this way purely out of necessity.



From the accounts of Mahaprabhu at Puri we learn that Govinda was engaged in his service until the close of his lila. If our conjecture is right, Govinda served Mahaprabhu for these 25 years, from 1508 to 1533 CE. Where was the home of this companion of so many years, whom the Vaishnavas honour with the name "Sri Govinda"? That he was a Bengali is certain, but no one has given any other particulars about him, and this too is very strange. The other companions all have their particulars given in the Vaishnava books. Of so great a devoted follower, and a loving Bengali at that, Vaishnava literature gives no address at all. Does this not lead one to infer that the disguise of that Govinda who kept his Karcha "in deep secrecy" (p. 62), who, having cut the bonds of maya at Kanchananagar, cherished no higher aim than to be allowed to serve Mahaprabhu's feet, and who, if our view of the Chaitanya-chandrodaya-kaumudi is right, hid the name of Kanchananagar and introduced himself as "a foreigner from Uttara Rarh", was deliberate, and that he concealed his own identity completely, just as he concealed the Karcha?

Compare the service of Sri Govinda with Govinda Dasa's service of Mahaprabhu in the South, and the doubt that the two are one person will be removed. Keeping track of food is one of the chief concerns of Govinda Dasa in the Karcha. It is the same with Sri Govinda at Puri. Compare these verses of the Chaitanya-charitamrita, Madhya ch. 14, 20; Madhya ch. 12, 101; Madhya ch. 12, 85; Antya ch. 7, 65; Antya ch. 10, 30–33, with such lines of the Karcha as "I shall get ... with a curry of banana flower" (p. 4), "How many fruits and roots ... juicy" (p. 4), "Greens, soup ... was" (p. 14), "Offering the bhoga ... mouth" (p. 14), "Prasada: 'Nimba-sukta ... king'" (p. 15), "Chinachur, khurma, laddu ... bak" (p. 20).

One charge is that certain events have been left out of the Karcha. At that time the roads were unsafe because of the constant wars between Vijayanagara and the Muslims. So perhaps they were not able to visit every holy place; that does not prove that errors have remained in the Karcha and that it is therefore unauthentic!

Copyists' errors are common in old manuscripts, and besides that, recovering the reading is often very difficult. Proper names in particular often cause much confusion. On the last page of Manik Ganguli's Dharma-mangala, published by the Sahitya Parishad, there is a strange word "gapherio". At one time several great heads were scratched trying to explain this word. Then the writer of this introduction was able to give an explanation of it and resolve the doubt. "Gapherio" is nothing but a corruption of "shake ritu" ["in the Shaka year ... ritu (a number-word)"], and  a laughable slip of the copyist. With geographical names, proper names and the like, guessing does not help, so copyists' errors are more frequent in them; and so if any mistake comes to light, it is not right to load them all on the author's shoulders.

The late Jaygopal Goswami, being unable in many places to make out the reading, put in words himself, and in some places, unable to understand a worm-eaten line, filled up that line himself; he told me this himself, and his eldest son Banwari Lal Goswami and his second son Mohan Lal Goswami have also stated it. When the Karcha was published Banwari Lal Goswami was 40 years old and helped his father. The places where such changes occurred he has pointed out so far as he remembers them, as has already been written; the present edition has been printed and published accordingly.

As has already been said, changes of this kind occurred in all the books of that time. In books such as the Ramayana, the Mahabharata, the Chandi and the Padakalpataru there is no end to such changes. The Charitamrita is the principal scripture of the Vaishnavas. Its text has been preserved with great care. So fewer changes are seen in it. Even so, there are a great many variant readings. Since all the books of that time underwent more or less change, why should the Karcha alone be held guilty of that sin? Those who know anything about handwritten manuscripts know that between a hundred-year-old manuscript and a two-hundred-year-old manuscript of the same book there are a great many variant readings and differences in narration.

Last of all, I have one request to make. In this introduction I have been obliged to subject great books such as the Chaitanya-charitamrita, the Chaitanya-bhagavata and the Chaitanya-chandrodaya to the judgement of my small intelligence. Weighed in the scales of history, the accounts in the books mentioned are not in every respect worthy of credence. These books are full of descriptions of divine lila. There is not so much inclination in them towards the human lila. Their chief aim is to establish that Chaitanya is Sri Krishna. That is why I have hesitated to value them as secular history.

But the Chaitanya-charitamrita and the Chaitanya-bhagavata are things of the highest glory in the Bengali language. These two great books are two pillars of fame of sixteenth-century Bengali literature. They are endowed with many virtues. As a mountain peak, though covered with dense jungle, dry leaves and broken rock, spreads its sky-touching majesty, so these two great books too, in spite of a number of historical flaws, shine bright with the majesty of the national theology of Bengal. The late Haradhan Datta Bhaktinidhi wrote of the Chaitanya-charitamrita: "The day I do not read this great book, that day is a day of misfortune." The hearts of many Vaishnavas will respond to these words.



The historical value of these two books too is not small. So if my writing contains any discussion of them, I have had to undertake it out of necessity, for the sake of historical criticism, not to wound the hearts of devotees. If I have done so through inadvertence, I beg their pardon. But have those who at every turn let fly arrows of ridicule at so great a devotee as Govinda Dasa no cause for remorse? For a Vaishnava, the offence of reviling a Vaishnava is very grave. In this connection I shall cite the impartial remark of the orthodox Vaishnava Achyutacharan Tattvanidhi. Referring to a Vaishnava devotee who reviles Govinda Dasa, he says with humility: "It is worth considering whether it is not a very bold thing for a devotee to do this, and whether a devotee is not being slighted thereby."

In the Karcha we see that when the Lord was in a trance Govinda Dasa guarded him with due vigilance. He knew that Mahaprabhu, when in his senses, kept himself especially apart from the company of women in order to teach people. But for fear that, if he went to the muraris, the intoxication of the Hari-nama might come over the Lord and rob him of his senses, he forbade Mahaprabhu to go there: "I said, there is no need to go to that place; Chaitanya Gosai did not heed my words" (p. 55). In the Chaitanya-charitamrita we see that when the Lord, hearing a song of Jayadeva from a devadasi's lips, ran in his frenzy to embrace her, this vigilant servant saved him (Antya, ch. 23 [as printed], 26).

In many respects the Govinda Dasa of the Karcha and Sri Govinda, the famous attendant at Puri, appear to be one person. The service of both is of the same stamp; both are shudras. The Govinda of the Karcha could not have been happy even in Vaikuntha apart from the Lord; if he survived the southern journey, he certainly never went anywhere away from Chaitanya. After the return from the South, Chaitanya sent him to Shantipur. At this short separation Govinda wept and was overcome. From the Chaitanya-chandrodaya-kaumudi it is clearly seen that Govinda went round by Srikhanda and Shantipur, joined Shivananda Sena's party and came back to Puri. Then Kavi Karnapura and Krishnadasa Kaviraja suddenly announce that a shudra Govinda, introducing himself as Ishvara Puri's servant, came to the Lord and remained his intimate companion for the rest of his life. Although Govinda served him for 25 years, no Vaishnava writer gives any information about his home or anything else (though many books certainly mention his devotion). On the other hand, we see that Govinda Dasa of the Karcha had a special  need to conceal himself; had he not done so, he would very probably have been forced to leave Mahaprabhu's company at Puri and play the "rotten householder" at Kanchananagar in Shashimukhi's company. The Karcha too hints at his concealing himself. Govinda Dasa described himself as a glutton, but this is only his Vaishnava simplicity and humility. That he had taken charge of collecting and storing provisions, however, is clearly seen in both books, the Karcha and the Charitamrita; and the accounts of this in the two books agree remarkably. His service and his devotion to Mahaprabhu are also of the same kind. Reading the two books one clearly gets the impression that the same Govinda who followed him like a shadow in the South and gathered food also followed him like a shadow in the temple at Puri and guarded the store of provisions. The same Govinda who forbade him to go to the quarter of the muraris saved him at Puri from the touch of the devadasi. So we are firmly convinced that these two persons are one. Otherwise it is not credible that so intimate a servant, on coming back from the South, should have dropped out of his company altogether and vanished. And yet at just that auspicious moment, when he particularly needed to conceal himself, a shudra named Govinda, introducing himself as Ishvara Puri's servant, suddenly became the Lord's most intimate companion. If this riddle is carefully examined, the identity of the two persons will clearly come to light. Govinda in his boundless humility and simplicity described himself as "the crest-jewel of gluttons"; Mahaprabhu himself in many places described himself as "low" and "untouchable" ("The Lord says: I beg alms at householders' doors; I am utterly untouchable, do not touch me," p. 55). To take such utterances of holy men in a bad sense and try to make them out guilty is altogether unjust. Govinda ate nothing but Mahaprabhu's prasada, "Unless it is prasada I do not eat" (p. 30), which means that he fasted many times along with Mahaprabhu. In the Bagula forest both fasted in this way for three days and three nights (p. 29). But Govinda felt no hardship in this fasting: "I feel no hunger or thirst by the Lord's grace; that is why I lie wherever I may be" (p. 50). Is a man who forgot hunger and thirst at the sight of Chaitanya's face a glutton?

Govinda was with Mahaprabhu until his disappearance. In the Antya section of the Chaitanya-charitamrita, ch. 10, verses 20, 31, we see that Govinda and Svarupa were alarmed at the frenzied state of Mahaprabhu's last years. At that time Mahaprabhu, though in the world, was no longer in the world. Having become part of the lila of Radha and Krishna, he had taken up residence in the Vrindavana of his heart. No outside word reached his ears any more. In the Antya section (ch. 17, verse 5)  we see that Mahaprabhu has run off somewhere at night from the Jagannatha temple and Govinda is searching for him like a madman.

Where Govinda went after Mahaprabhu's disappearance has not been traced. But it does not seem likely that so intimate a servant, whose whole life was in Mahaprabhu, lived long after being parted from his heavenly company.

[^00b-11]: [Quotations from the Chaitanya-chandrodaya-kaumudi:] "To Narahari Dasa and all the devotees, he came and bowed at their feet." [Continued in a footnote on p. 73:] "Narahari, embracing him, asked: where is your home, on what business are you going? Govinda said: my home is in Uttara Rarh."

## A Few More Words about the Karcha

Although we regard Govinda's Karcha as an authoritative work on the life of Mahaprabhu in every respect, we set down a few of our views for the reader's information.

It cannot be said that Govinda Dasa is always free from error. He may not have had the opportunity to write the Karcha every day; on the road there were times when he had to spend many days in the jungle. Often he travelled amid all sorts of hardships, and in such conditions it is not impossible that he wrote up the Karcha every 10 or 15 days. In the meantime his memory of small matters may have grown dim, and so some mistakes have crept in. For the early part of the Karcha he kept some notes, which he afterwards turned into payar verse. That is why the early part is very brief. On the southern journey, having plenty of leisure, he turned his notes into payar in solitude as he made them or immediately afterwards, and so the account of that period is very lively and engaging.

The Karcha is brief, and so many things may have been left out of it. Mahaprabhu, travelling in that country, learnt Tamil and Telugu ("Now Gora Raya speaks in the Tamil tongue, now he explains to people speaking Sanskrit. Having wandered long in this land, Shachi's darling understands everyone's speech," p. 62). So Govinda Dasa from time to time asked the Lord about various things and jotted them down. But he could not understand everything.

For those who have no use for history, Achyuta Babu has set up a proof. That proof may not be acceptable to everyone, but to an orthodox Vaishnava it will count for more than many historical proofs. Achyuta Babu writes: "Over and above all these proofs there is one more proof, a spiritual proof. I will not name him, but we know that a highly educated devotee, a follower of Gaura, in a state of meditation directly beheld certain places and lilas as described in this Karcha of Govinda, not in a dream or a swoon, but awake. What more can be said  about the authenticity of this Karcha of Govinda?" (Sri Sri Vishnupriya-Gauranga Patrika, year 3, no. 5, p. 160).

We would not have cited this proof. History has no room for proofs of this kind. But it shows that even among orthodox Vaishnavas there is no lack of people who hold the Karcha in special reverence and even practise meditation with this book. The campaign of Jogendra Mohan Ghosh, a follower of Pandit Rasik Mohan Vidyabhushan, though on a very large scale, is confined to very few people. In this age the business of collecting signatures has become so cheap that it has no particular value. Still less do the big tilakas and the tokens of Bhagavata learning of those who know nothing of the Bengali language, who believe in supernatural lilas more than in reality, and who take no interest in history, count for anything with us. Well-known historians of the present day have acknowledged the authenticity of this Karcha; of them I shall name two: Sriyukta Rakhaldas Bandyopadhyay and Manomohan Chakravarti[^00b-12]. Some among the opponents may be learned, but none of them has any concern with history.

The objector in the Prabasi has put forward another argument: that at that time the Karmakars [smiths] were wholly illiterate. Long ago, among the Theris of the Buddhist age, we find the name of a learned woman of the Karmakar caste. But we have obtained a copy of Kavikankana's Chandi written a little later than Govinda Dasa's time and found in Kanchananagar itself; on its last leaf it is written that a man of that place named Ramjiban Karmakar won fame by following the profession of reciting the Chandi and other poems. This manuscript is with me at present. Sriyukta Harshit Keshari Ray of 9 Chintamani Das Lane lent it to me to see.

The last two lines of the Karcha are: "How shall I bear the arrow of separation from the Lord? Cruel anguish has arisen in my mind." Thus is Govinda's state of mind described just before he was to take Chaitanya's letter to Advaita at Shantipur.

Whether the book ends here or whether there was more after this is not known.



[^00b-12]: A "certain distinguished Vaishnava", greatly upset at Manomohan Babu's acknowledging the Karcha as an authentic work, wrote in the Anandabazar Patrika of Friday, 8 Kartik 1331: "Even the antiquarian and great scholar Sriyukta Manomohan Chakravarti has accepted it (the Karcha) as an authentic work." Alas!!!

## Why Did the Karcha Remain Hidden So Long?

If Govinda had died immediately after returning from the South, the Karcha would have been found when the things he left behind were searched, and so valuable a history would have become known at once.

If, after coming back, Govinda had returned to his home at Kanchananagar, he would have had no reason left to hide either himself or the Karcha. In that case too the Karcha would have become well known.

We have already given the one reason why this book should have remained hidden. He concealed the Karcha out of longing to be Chaitanya's companion for ever; fearing that he might lose that company and be forced to return home, he must have blotted out his own identity entirely.

Govinda felt no need to describe the later events of Mahaprabhu's life, because after returning to Puri Mahaprabhu was surrounded by many learned and gifted men, who were eager to record the events of his life. Govinda had only a slight acquaintance with Bengali letters. So, among them, he did not presume to take up the pen.

Moreover, if he had gone on writing after this, his Karcha and his identity would have been discovered among Lord Chaitanya's associates. The need that Govinda Dasa had felt when Mahaprabhu had few companions and was travelling alone far from Bengal no longer existed later on.

So it seems to me that the published Karcha is not incomplete. Perhaps, having written up to that point, Govinda went no further.

But who untied the string with which he had tied up his Karcha, and after how long, is not known.

## Our Explanation

If anyone points out faults in my books and essays, I accept them at once. In historical inquiry mistakes and slips are inevitable. As language and literature progressively flourish, it is desirable to correct the mistaken notions of the past. I have not so lost my wits as to cling to my mistakes and stubbornly maintain my position. But even so, in spite of the errors of the Karcha being proclaimed with so much beating of drums, I have not been able to see  that my view of the Karcha is mistaken. In the Pallivasi of 13 Magh 1332 someone has written: "We have questioned one or two of his (Dinesh Babu's) students, and he himself is not free from doubt about this Karcha, yet a dreadful prestige or vain pride has taken such hold of him that he cannot by any means admit the truth."

What more shall I write about the many shapes in which falsehood is appearing in the discussion of this Karcha? Govinda Dasa's Karcha has been my inseparable companion for 30 years; hundreds and hundreds of my tears have fallen on its every page. As a lotus in bloom fills every quarter with fragrance, so the story of Mahaprabhu given in the Karcha is full of his heavenly love and the sweetness of his lila. The day I first read this book was a memorable day for me. The echo of the song of divine lila that sounded in my ears that day is ringing still. The true form of Lord Chaitanya that the Karcha has shown me I have found nowhere else. Elsewhere, amid attempts to establish complicated doctrines of avatars and amid mystifications, only glimpses of Mahaprabhu's life are to be had. Like a flash of lightning gleaming for a moment in a bank of clouds, that glimpse vanishes the next moment into the fog of every kind of display of learning and the doctrine of avatars. But look just once at this madman of love in the Karcha. In this little book he seems to have revealed himself entirely. To me this book is the altar at the foot of Mahaprabhu's footstool. That I have doubts about this book is an utter falsehood, unworthy of credit.

I am not an orthodox Vaishnava, not even a Vaishnava at all: I am a Shakta. I am not a Karmakar, nor a Kayastha; I am a Vaidya. I have no interest in this faction-fight of Karmakars and Kayasthas. I am not concerned to understand the Lord's supernatural lilas; I am merely studying the history of his life. So for what interest of mine should I make it a point of honour to cling to a false opinion about this book? In other books there are attempts to dress him up by attributing supernatural divine power to him, but here I see his true portrait. "Kim api hi madhuranam mandanam nakritinam" ["What is not an ornament to lovely forms?"]. Must a lotus be adorned? Must a rose be adorned? Is the beauty of the hundred-petalled lotus increased by showing it encircled by serpents? Is not the garb of a destroyer on the avatar of love a self-evident blemish? Reading this introduction of mine you will understand with how much heartfelt love I have savoured this book; will you find anything of obstinacy in it?

Moreover, among the Karmakars of Kanchananagar there is not one disciple of the late Jaygopal Goswami. In former times people would write a book and pass it off under the name of some great author,  and the book would thereby circulate more widely. In this way many shastras were circulated under the name of Krishna Dvaipayana Vedavyasa. What prestige would Gosainji have added to the book, and what glory would he have added to himself, by writing his own composition under the name of a blacksmith! When Moti Babu and his colleagues of the Amrita Bazar Patrika declared 51 of the 227 pages of the previous edition of this book to be doubtful, Moti Babu himself wrote: "With what intention the writer wrote this supernatural part we do not know" (Sri Vishnupriya Patrika, Gauranga era 410, month of Kartik). In fact the Goswami had no intention whatever of making out a Kayastha to be a Karmakar. That Mahaprabhu had a companion named Govinda Karmakar, neither he nor anyone else had the faintest inkling at that time. Afterwards, when the name of Govinda Karmakar was found in an old manuscript of the Chaitanya-mangala, that agitation died out completely.

Although in this introduction we have given various external proofs in demonstrating the authenticity of the Karcha, I myself have no need whatever of all these proofs. As, when one stands before a fire, one knows by its heat that the fire is there even with one's eyes shut, so the wondrous intoxication of love in this book is to me the great witness of its authenticity. The soul-stirring divine portrait of Mahaprabhu that Govinda has drawn is its chief witness. The geographical picture he has given is its chief witness. The portrait in this book is a photograph of that extraordinary god of love, messenger of the heavenly world; no one could draw it by learning, by devotion or by his own invention.

The objectors have resorted to many falsehoods, but I shall say in the words of Kashidasa: "How long does a tilaka of water stay on the forehead? How long does a stone stay in the sky when it is thrown up?"

## Chaitanya's Religious Convictions

It is quite true that Chaitanya is neither a Shakta nor a Vaishnava, belongs neither to the householders nor to the sannyasis: he belongs to all.

It cannot be said that the way the Vaishnavas have understood him is the only right one. He himself never described himself as an avatar; with boundless humility he declared again and again that he was a man. What some writers have described, on the strength of things he said or did in a trance, in order to establish that he was an avatar, seems to us exaggerated and incredible. There is not a single statement of that kind in the Karcha. On the contrary, what the Chaitanya-bhagavata writes, that in his presence no one could  call him anything but a servant of God and get away with it (Chaitanya-bhagavata, Antya, 10), seems to be the truth.

Though he received initiation in the Madhva sampradaya, he preserved the independence of his own views. Nowhere did he preach unconditional faith in the guru. On the contrary, if Rama Raya was one of the foremost of his devotees, and if his views can be accepted as Chaitanya's views, then it seems that he did not accept the doctrine of the guru [guruvada]. Rama Raya's famous song says:

> "I sought no go-between, I sought no other:  
> at the meeting of us two the go-between was the Five-arrowed One [Kama]."

What is the meaning of this verse? Does it not plainly hint that between the devotee and God there is no guru or third person? In the Karcha this is put still more plainly:

> "Faith itself brings one to God and unites one with him."

From this it is clearly seen that God draws the hearts of people himself and does not do so through representatives. He [Chaitanya] himself made no one his disciple.

But is the array of avatars that was constructed out of his associates in later times, when he came to be regarded as the incarnation of the united form of Radha and Krishna, correct? Would he have approved of it? Every associate, and even later devotees, came to be counted as incarnations of the gopis of Vraja and of various gods. It is they who are the owners of the Vrindavana lila. No one has the right to understand love for Krishna by going over their heads. Many Sanskrit and Bengali books, such as the Gaura-ganoddesha, have been written about these avatars. The keys of the storehouse of that religion of love to which the whole world has a right are held by these incarnations of gopis and their descendants. In this age the lila of Chaitanya will have to be understood anew. The age of the domination of mullahs and priestcraft has come to an end. All the fancies of supernatural lila, all the rubbish, will have to be cleared away and the religion that Lord Chaitanya taught explained in its purity. And even if the supernatural lilas were true, what of it? Beside the wondrous ideal that Mahaprabhu displayed through his boundless humility and endless tears, these supernatural lilas have no value; they are a means of beguiling idle people, and educated people will not accept them. So those who have set a guard of shlokas over the Vaishnava religion and keep it under close watch need not trouble with their guardianship. Chaitanya's religion is much greater than the formulas they have devised. We shall not be taken in by tilakas and body-paint, nor be frightened by the profusion of "Sri"s and "Srila"s before names. If they try to clear away the rubbish, we too shall try from the other side to clear away the rubbish and make the truth shine forth. Now the simple heart of the matter will prevail over empty learning, actual events over cock-and-bull stories and blind superstition, and the human lila acceptable to all over the divine lila of the Puranas, attracting greater reverence;  and in this endeavour to clear the rubbish from the Vaishnava religion it will be necessary to rely less on the Meru and Mandara of medieval learning than on the flowing, clear and pure stream of history, such as the Karcha.

Finally, I must say that everything written here about Govinda Dasa's Karcha is my own opinion, and neither the University nor anyone else is responsible for it. But I believe that judicious readers of this introduction will come round to my view. Many large-hearted Prabhupada Goswamis too have shown their nobility by stepping beyond the bounds of their own narrow interests. We express our boundless respect to them. A great deal of labour has gone into editing this book. But we shall not take a cowrie for it. 250 copies of the book will be distributed free and the rest at cost price only.

> 7 Vishvakosh Lane, Bagbazar, Calcutta, 19 July 1926  
> Sri Dinesh Chandra Sen

---

# Part 1

*Navadvipa. Govinda comes to Mahaprabhu; the decision to take sannyasa; the night of departure (pp. 1–20)*

*[p. 1]*

**1.** My home is in Kanchananagar, in Burdwan.
My father's name is Shyamadasa; my name is Govinda.

**2.** By caste I am a smith: I forge weapons, ladles and fetters.
My mother's name was Madhavi.

**3.** My wife's name is Shashimukhi.
One day, quarrelling, she spoke harshly to me —

**4.** she abused me as a worthless fool.
Stung by that insult, I left home at dawn.

**5.** I set out in the Shaka year 1430,[^01-1]
seething with wounded pride, and never looked back.

**6.** Step by step I reached Katwa,
and there I heard the name of Sri Chaitanya.

**7.** Everyone was praising Chaitanya,
and hearing it I hurried off to see him.

*[p. 2]*

**8.** All day I walked across the fields,
and at dawn I crossed over and came to the landing of Nadia.

**9.** Below Nadia, on the Ganga, is a landing called Mishraghat.
My joy grew as I beheld the capital of Nadia.[^01-2]

**10.** On the right the river Vagdevi murmurs along,
as if singing for the joy of all.

**11.** Above the landing is the courtyard of Srivasa,
and near it a huge tank.

**12.** Close by stands the palace of King Ballala;
it lies in ruins, yet its traces still remain.

**13.** I sat on the landing, lost in thought for a long while,
and just then Sri Chaitanya came to bathe.

**14.** A gamchha[^01-3] tied at his waist, his frame finely built,
and with him a joyful Avadhuta.

**15.** Three or four more companions came along, dancing;
the Lord went down into the bosom of the Ganga to bathe.

**16.** The heroic Avadhuta leapt in from the bank
and began to sport in the water, swimming.

**17.** Behind them came Srivasa Thakura and Damodara,
Siddha Haridasa, and on the left Gadadhara.

**18.** Last of all came Advaita Gosai.
Never had I seen anyone so majestic.

*[p. 3]*

**19.** White hair and grey beard, wonderfully winning;
his beard reached below his chest.

**20.** The old man swam, shouting the name of Hari,
while the Avadhuta swam back and forth across the river.

**21.** One by one they all plunged into the Ganga
and, swimming, began all sorts of games.

**22.** But I gazed at the Lord's wondrous form,
enchanted by its radiance.

**23.** Having bathed, Gora-chand[^01-4] came up onto the bank,
his curling locks tumbling down his back.

**24.** His body was the colour of pure gold,
his eyes long like the petals of a blue lotus.

**25.** Lovely cheeks, a broad forehead;
even his ordinary walk looked like a dancer's step.

**26.** His charming thighs outshine plantain stems,
and his two lovely brows seem drawn with a brush.

**27.** His two feet look as though painted with alta;[^01-5]
whoever sees them, even a sage's eyes are entranced.

**28.** His body full of love, "Hari bol!" on his lips,
he embraces whomever he favours with mercy.

**29.** My Gora says "Hari" and sheds tears;
the tears spurt out like jets from a squirt-gun.

*[p. 4]*

**30.** Then my Gora Raya set off,
and the Avadhuta Nityananda ran after him.

**31.** A fisherman told me who they were,
and one by one I came to know them all.

**32.** As I watched their water-play with my own eyes,
an ocean of feeling surged up in my heart.

**33.** People said God had come to Shachi's house,
and that is why my heart was so eager to see him.

**34.** By good fortune the quarrel at home was only a pretext;
that is why I hastened to Navadvipa.

**35.** Sitting on the landing, I watched this lila,
and who knows what feeling arose in me.

**36.** My body bristled like a kadamba flower,
and I began to tremble all over.

**37.** I broke into a sweat and my cloth grew wet;
I longed to wash his feet with my tears.

**38.** With curling locks down his back and a smiling face,
the Lord came on, surrounded by his companions.

**39.** As luck would have it, the Lord noticed me
and glanced at me sidelong again and again.

**40.** And when he came slowly nearer,
I fell to the ground and clasped his feet.

*[p. 5]*

**41.** I rolled at his feet,
and merciful Nimai took my hand and sat me up.

**42.** I sat before him with folded hands, weeping,
and smiling and laughing he asked me a word or two.

**43.** Nectar flowed unceasingly in his smile,
and the fragrance of his body enchanted my heart.

**44.** Ever intoxicated with the Name of Hari, very slender;
so many devotees were rolling at his feet.

**45.** How can I describe what that feeling was?
It seemed that millions of gods came to fall at his feet.

**46.** When the Lord stands in a dark room,
the lustre of his body dispels the darkness.

**47.** What creator, sitting in solitude,
must have moulded him by kneading the moon with streams of nectar?

**48.** Whoever sees this form with his own eyes,
detachment from worldly things follows at his heels.

**49.** Smiling, he talked with me
and kindly asked my name.

**50.** The Lord said: "What is your caste, what is your name,
what trade do you follow, where is your home?"

**51.** Hearing the Lord's words, I folded my hands
and, forgetting myself, began to speak.

*[p. 6]*

**52.** "O merciful one, why such kindness to me?
The name of this lowly one is Govinda Dasa.

**53.** I was a householder, working at many things,
but now I have become a beggar on the road.

**54.** I have left worldly life to see the Lord;
now give me a place at your crimson feet.

**55.** My home is in Kanchananagar, in Burdwan;
my father's name is Shyamadasa Karmakar."

**56.** Hearing this, the Lord said to me:
"Stay, Govinda, in my house.

**57.** In my house you will be looked after,
and every day you will joyfully sing the sankirtana of the Name.

**58.** Every day you will happily receive Krishna's prasada,
and every wish of your heart will be fulfilled.

**59.** You will always be engaged in service:
bring Ganga water and tulasi.

**60.** Every day you will eat prasada to your fill —
juicy greens, shukta, and ghanta of banana flower."[^01-6]

**61.** Saying this, the Lord wanted to take me with him,
and at once I went into the Lord's household.

**62.** The house stands above the Ganga, most charming:
five handsome thatched huts.

*[p. 7]*

**63.** The Lord lives at the southern edge of the town,
intoxicated with the Name of Hari, always rejoicing.

**64.** Near it is a huge tank,
which some call the Ballala-sagara.

**65.** Let me now name, one by one,
the devotees who are always with the Lord:

**66.** Advaita Acharya, and also Svarupa and Srivasa;
the Acharya's two sons, Achyuta and Krishnadasa;[^01-7]

**67.** Mukunda, Murari Gupta and Gadadhara;
Narahari, Vidyanidhi, Shekhara, Shridhara;

**68.** and two or four more intimate devotees
with whom he worships in secret.

**69.** The Avadhuta Nityananda is like a madman,
rolling on the ground and shedding tears without end.

**70.** Shachi-devi is gentleness itself, very short of stature;
she is forever calling out, "Nimai! Nimai!"

**71.** Vishnupriya-devi is the Lord's wife,
busy day and night with his service.

**72.** Modest, humble, soft and gentle of speech;
I became the servant of her feet.

**73.** So I lived as a servant in Shachi's house,
finishing every task before I was asked.

*[p. 8]*

**74.** I am an expert eater and eat with delight;
after doing the Lord's work I go about with him.

**75.** Every day bhoga is offered in the Vishnu temple:
so many fruits and roots, curd-cheese, butter, cream and thickened milk!

**76.** Greens, dal, yogurt, shukta, modakas, payasa,
vadas, laddus and sweets of every kind — delicious to eat.

**77.** Every day Mother Shachi does the cooking,
and everyone joyfully honours the prasada.

**78.** I, this servant, am the crown-jewel of gluttons;
all year round I eat from the merciful Lord's plate.

**79.** What can I say? Prasada has no equal;
a single grain of it is like nectar.

**80.** Thus I stayed in the Lord's house,
and everyone was kind to me as Chaitanya's servant.

**81.** My Lord is Chaitanya Gosai;
wherever and whenever he goes, I go with him.

**82.** His heart is always restless with love for Krishna;
when he hears Krishna's name, tears stream down.

**83.** If anyone calls out "Radhe!" aloud,
at once his tears pour down in torrents.

**84.** If someone happens to call "Pranakrishna!",[^01-8]
he runs to him and embraces him.

*[p. 9]*

**85.** One evening, in Srivasa's courtyard,
the Lord was sitting with his devotees.

**86.** Just then my Avadhuta Raya
began to cry out again and again, "Yamuna!

**87.** Here is the site of the rasa, the landing of the Yamuna!
Where are the hundreds of gopis, where is that dance?"

**88.** Hearing Nitai's words, the Lord rushed off
and leapt into the Ballala-sagara.

**89.** Overwhelmed with feeling, the Lord swam,
while all the devotees stood on the bank watching.

**90.** Thus his longing grew day by day,
and under the weight of prema his body grew thin.

**91.** But merciful Chaitanya was not satisfied with this.
He said, "I will take sannyasa and teach the living beings.

**92.** With a straw between my teeth[^01-9] I will go round all the villages
and deliver every living being by giving the Name of Hari.

**93.** Abandoning household life, I will go to the town of Katwa;
my guru Keshava Bharati will set me free.

**94.** I will not stay at home; I will take sannyasa —
otherwise how shall I deliver all beings?"

**95.** The Lord wished to take sannyasa;
hearing it, I felt a great pain in my heart.

*[p. 10]*

**96.** The Lord called the Avadhuta and said:
"I shall take sannyasa — do not stop me.

**97.** At the holy time, in the month of Magha, on the sun's northern course,[^01-10]
I shall take sannyasa. Keep this secret.

**98.** Tell this to Mukunda and Gadadhara,
but do not proclaim it everywhere.

**99.** Tell my mother only by a hint,
and do not announce it among the devotees."

**100.** I, the servant who was with him, heard it all,
and my heart seemed to split in two.

**101.** Alas, alas! This grief cannot be borne —
my Lord Gora Raya will take sannyasa!

**102.** Gora will go away to take sannyasa!
Let my heart burst into a hundred pieces!

**103.** I am lighter than a straw; what does it matter to me?
And yet let a hundred thunderbolts fall on my head!

**104.** How shall I bear the pain of separation from the Lord?
How shall I pass my days without Chaitanya?

**105.** After that the Lord himself rose
and went to Mukunda.

**106.** I followed him weeping,
unable to see the road for my tears.

*[p. 11]*

**107.** Calling Mukunda, he said:
"I shall accept the staff and the water-pot.[^01-11]

**108.** Giving up my shikha and sacred thread, I shall take sannyasa;
if I do not, how shall I deliver the living beings?"

**109.** Hearing these words, the worthy Mukunda
flooded his broad chest with tears.

**110.** Mukunda collapsed to the ground,
and the Lord took him by the hand, raised him and sat him down.

**111.** "My life is leaving me — what have you told me, O merciful one?
Hearing these words, this wretch's heart is breaking.

**112.** Go on distributing the Name of Hari a while longer,
and then, Lord, renounce the world and take sannyasa."

**113.** Hearing this, the Lord hurried to Gadadhara
and told him everything openly.

**114.** Hearing the words, Gadadhara cried out,
as though the sky had fallen on his head.

**115.** Gadadhara rolled on the ground,
and his body turned red with streams of blood.

**116.** "What have you told me?" said Gadadhara, rising and sitting up.
"Your … [illegible] heart.

**117.** Bring me poison, I will drink it at once!
Oh, how shall I bear separation from the Lord?

*[p. 12]*

**118.** If millions of scorpions were to sting me,
that pain would be trifling compared with this.

**119.** If my beloved Nimai renounces everything,
I shall follow him in love.

**120.** When Murari and the other devotees hear of this,
they will fall senseless wherever they are.

**121.** If Chaitanya leaves us, what use is the body?
Let a thunderbolt fall on our heads this very moment!"

**122.** Then Gadadhara clasped his feet
and, shedding tears, began to say:

**123.** "When your mother hears this,
how will she keep the life in her body?"

**124.** Afterwards, as the news passed from ear to ear,
alas, the very life of the Vaishnavas seemed to fly away.

**125.** One said, "Millions of scorpions are stinging me!"
Another said, "My life is burning in fire!"

**126.** Some fell like felled trees,
others, with clenched teeth, collapsed senseless.

**127.** Hearing all this, my Vishvambhara
began to reason with everyone at length.

**128.** The Lord hastened to the Vaishnavas
and consoled them all with sweet words.

*[p. 13]*

**129.** Then Shachi-devi heard this news
and, sensing disaster, fell unconscious.

**130.** Beating her breast, Shachi wept aloud,
her tears falling on her breast.

**131.** "Oh Nimai, where will you go, my son?"
Hearing her lament, even beasts and birds wept.

**132.** Then the Avadhuta came into the Lord's courtyard
and told this to Shachi.

**133.** Like a thunderbolt the words pierced Shachi's heart,
and at once she fell crashing to the ground.

**134.** Striking her breast she wept loudly,
and the earth turned to mud with the stream of her tears.

**135.** Vishnupriya, hearing the whispered news,
sat down at once, her hands on her head.

**136.** Tears streamed down over her breast,
as if an ocean of grief had surged up.

**137.** But Gora paid no heed to her
and went off to Srivasa's courtyard.

**138.** There, in Srivasa's house, the Lord began
a great sankirtana and lost outer consciousness.

**139.** Intoxicated with kirtana, Gora began to dance,
and his cloth slipped off.

*[p. 14]*

**140.** His body became like a kadamba flower;[^01-12]
my hero of dharma danced, unconscious of himself.

**141.** Streams of blood oozed from his pores,
his body bruised from his falls.

**142.** As he danced he cried, "O Vanamali!"
and the devotees danced with him, clapping their hands.

**143.** On the sankranti of Pausha,[^01-13] at dusk,
the Lord came back to his own home.

**144.** Many people kept coming and going,
and Mother Shachi's grief welled up in waves.

**145.** He consoled his mother with sweet words
and then showed himself in the kitchen.

**146.** The second watch of the night passed;
the Lord ate and lay down to sleep.

**147.** I too went to my own place and lay down,
but at the Lord's order I stayed awake.

**148.** In the last part of the night the merciful Lord
came out, called me and said:

**149.** "Be ready and wait here;
I will go and take leave at my mother's feet."

**150.** So saying he went into the inner apartments,
then came out again and called me.

*[p. 15]*

**151.** Urgently he said, "Come with me.
Let us go to Katwa to cut the bonds.

**152.** Do not tell this to anyone and everyone:
I will take sannyasa and deliver the living beings.

**153.** Selfish and wicked, they drink wine and eat meat —
tell me, what will become of the people of Kali?

**154.** Devoted to belly and genitals, without any discipline,
they lie incessantly for the sake of money.

**155.** Worms of lust, they lap the spittle of women's mouths
and throw away the stream of the nectar of bhakti.

**156.** They relish the food of harlots and obey harlots,
absorbed in gold, women, arts and amorous play.

**157.** Therefore I will give up my shikha and thread
and go from door to door giving the Name of Hari.

**158.** The great mantra of the Name requires no initiation;
I will preach that Name on roads and at landings.

**159.** Chandalas, youths, householders, children, the old and women,
drunk with the Name, will stand in rows.

**160.** Boys will say "Hari", and girls will say it too;
even the godless and the Aghorapanthis will be drunk with the Name.

**161.** The banner of the Name will fly, piercing the sky;
king and subjects together will roll on the ground.

*[p. 16]*

**162.** If I do not take sannyasa and put on the kaupina,
how shall I deliver the sinful, the afflicted and the wretched?

**163.** Seeing the miserable state of the people of Kali,
I can stay no longer; my heart trembles.

**164.** Taking water-pot and kaupina I will become a sannyasi
and deliver everyone by giving the names of Radha and Krishna.

**165.** For those who are the worst sinners and sufferers
my heart is always weeping in anguish."

**166.** As he was speaking with me thus,
Shachi-devi appeared.

**167.** Shachi-devi came hurrying out
and stood before him with arms outstretched.

**168.** Then, clasping his mother's feet,
the Lord took leave and set off.

**169.** His mother stood in the doorway weeping,
and I followed behind, carrying his wooden sandals.

**170.** Shachi stood like a wooden doll,
tears pouring down in torrents.

**171.** Then, going out through the door,
my hero of dharma crossed the Ganga.

**172.** Having crossed, the Lord went on to Kantaka-nagara,[^01-14]
and I followed behind to serve him.

*[p. 17]*

**173.** All the wonderful lilas I am able to witness
I record in my karcha as far as my powers allow.[^01-15]

**174.** At dusk we reached Kantaka-nagara;
in every house gongs, conches and bells were sounding.

**175.** Then, during the night, Mukunda, Shekhara,
the Avadhuta, Brahmananda and Gadadhara,

**176.** the teacher Gangadasa and the singer Shivai —
one after another they all appeared.

**177.** In the depth of the night Gora, crying "Hari!",
began to dance, mad with love.

**178.** Hundreds of thousands of people came to see him,
and all were amazed at his devotion to Krishna.

**179.** Some threw flowers at him, some offered garlands;
the radiance of the Lord's body lit up every direction.

**180.** Millions of Cupids cannot compare with his beauty;
his waist is slimmer than the middle of a damaru drum.

**181.** Wherever his large eyes are turned,
blue lotuses seem to rain down there.

**182.** Arms reaching to his knees, very tall;
with a straw in his teeth, Gora begs for servitude to God.

**183.** Thus the night passed in dance and song.
Seeing the crowds, Gora began to speak:

*[p. 18]*

**184.** "Brothers, listen attentively to my words:
there is no difference at all between Krishna and Krishna's name.

**185.** Worship Krishna, think of Krishna, say Krishna's name;
by the power of the Name, brothers, you will reach the eternal abode.

**186.** All that you see here is false,
a mere shadow of prakriti — so say the Vedas.

**187.** Your cherished body will be left lying
when Yama comes and squeezes your throat.

**188.** There is no difference between a bedstead and a pallet on the ground;
those who see difference are the ones who suffer.

**189.** Whoever grows proud on getting wealth
is a worm of hell — such is the verdict of scripture.

**190.** Between king and pauper there is no difference at all;
it is ignorance, brothers, that creates the sense of difference.

**191.** A handful of rice fills a king's belly,
and with that same handful the poor man is content.

**192.** The penniless man sleeps happily on the bare ground,
while the king has no sleep on his priceless bed.

**193.** The king does not eat gold, diamonds, emeralds or pearls,
yet, drunk with wealth, he does not think of the Lord of all.

**194.** As the poor man will die at the hour of death,
so will the landlord go to the house of Yama.

*[p. 19]*

**195.** Over the king's eyes the blinkers of maya are tied;
he is always blind, like an oil-mill bullock.

**196.** The oil-mill bullock walks round and round in one place;
in millions of years its road never ends.

**197.** When the soul-bird flies away, the body will remain inert,
rattling like a broken cage.

**198.** The pampered body will rot and decay,
and jackals and dogs will eat their fill of it.

**199.** Drunk with pride and sunk in worldly life,
man dies brooding, crying "my property, my property".

**200.** Daughters, sons, mansions, tanks and gardens,
women, gold and the like rob him of his senses.

**201.** Whose daughter, whose son, whose husband?
Give up all inert things and fix your mind on Krishna.

**202.** False are sons, false are daughters, false are wealth and grain;
the one true reality is Chaitanya.[^01-16]

**203.** What more can I say about the rotten life of the householder?
He is worn to shreds, sunk in sons, daughters and riches.

**204.** As his property grows, so do his schemes,
and he suffers endless torment like a worm in filth.

**205.** Everywhere Krishna's form is shining,
but only one whose eyes are pure can see it.

*[p. 20]*

**206.** The fool attached to worldly things sees with fleshly eyes;
the eternally liberated devotee sees with the eye of divine knowledge.

**207.** How can one whose eyes are blinded by the dust of worldliness
manage to see subtle truth?

**208.** Everyone talks of love, love — but who knows what love is?
Is the essence of love the service of a woman?

**209.** When you know that man and woman are not different,
then the truth of love will surely dawn on you.

**210.** If love exists for the sake of offspring,
then there is no truth of love in it at all.

**211.** But if one feels in one's heart, 'I exist for God',
that the great souls certainly call love.

**212.** Hear attentively the truth of pure love,
whose slightest wave soothes a scorched heart.

**213.** Like the longing of a young woman on seeing a young man —
think as I may, I see no other longing like it.

**214.** That is why devotees worship the Lord of the Yadus,
fixing their minds on him in the mood of a wife.

**215.** But in one who longs for Atmarama,[^01-17]
can lust remain in the heart?

**216.** As darkness cannot remain near light,
so lust is burnt to ashes near Krishna."

---

### Translator's notes

[^01-1]: Shaka 1430 corresponds to 1508–09 CE. The traditional date of Chaitanya's sannyasa is the month of Magha, Shaka 1431 (January–February 1510).
[^01-2]: *Pat* is an old word for a capital (*raj-pat*). Mishraghat, the river Vagdevi and Ballala's tank (Ballala-sagara, Ballal Dighi) are local Navadvipa place-names.
[^01-3]: A *gamchha* is a thin cotton towel worn as a wrap.
[^01-4]: *Gora-chand*, "the golden moon", an affectionate name of Chaitanya.
[^01-5]: *Alta*: the red dye with which Bengali women paint the edges of their feet.
[^01-6]: *Shukta* is a slightly bitter Bengali vegetable stew; *ghanta* (*ghonto*) is a mixed dish, here made of banana flowers.
[^01-7]: Achyuta (Achyutananda) and Krishnadasa (Krishna Mishra) were sons of Advaita Acharya; they are central figures of the *Advaita-prakasha*.
[^01-8]: "Pranakrishna" means "Krishna, my life"; here apparently an ordinary personal name to which Chaitanya responds in ecstasy.
[^01-9]: Holding a straw between the teeth is a gesture of utmost humility.
[^01-10]: *Uttarayana*, the "northern course" of the sun after the winter solstice, an auspicious period.
[^01-11]: The *danda* (staff) and *kamandalu* (water-pot) are the emblems of a sannyasi; the *shikha* (tuft of hair) and sacred thread are given up on taking sannyasa.
[^01-12]: The kadamba flower is a fluffy ball; the image is of hair standing on end, one of the bodily signs of ecstasy.
[^01-13]: The sankranti of Pausha (Makara-sankranti) is the last day of the month of Pausha, in mid-January.
[^01-14]: Kantaka-nagara is the old name of Katwa.
[^01-15]: A key statement for the whole book: the *karcha* (*kadacha*) is a set of notes, a diary, which the author claims to keep as events unfold.
[^01-16]: A play on words: *chaitanya* means "consciousness, spirit", and is also Chaitanya's name.
[^01-17]: *Atmarama*, "he who delights in himself", an epithet of Krishna.

---

# Part 2

*Katwa: the sannyasa. Shantipur. Burdwan and Shashimukhi. On through Medinipur and Narayangarh (pp. 21–40)*

*[p. 21]*

**217.** Only the longing of love remains —
thus I have shown you where love is to be found.

**218.** Now strive with all your heart for love:
you will be fulfilled, and the torments of worldly life will go.

**219.** Through quarrels, disputes, hatred and lies
worldly life becomes a hell — think on this.

**220.** For money the householder tells lies,
cheats, and even kills wherever he can.

**221.** What more can I say of the householder's rotten life?
He is worn out by sons, daughters, possessions and wealth.

**222.** You never think whose you are and who is yours;
for the sake of an inert lump of flesh you are busy earning.

**223.** Death will surely come, there are no two ways about it,
yet you keep thinking you will live forever.

**224.** However many shastras you have read, combing every detail,
by reading you have all become nothing but great fools.

**225.** The more learning and cleverness, the more selfishness;
the more you read, the dirtier the heart becomes.

**226.** With your lips you call another's wife "mother",
but find her alone and at once you are blinded by lust.

**227.** Lust and anger are enemies when it comes to others,
but when it comes to yourself they are your friends.

*[p. 22]*

**228.** All these are the boundless torments of hell;
think about it in your heart, even once.

**229.** Until devotion to God arises,
so long will you remain in such a hell.

**230.** A small self-interest you can give up,
but millions of coins can lead you astray.

**231.** For the people of Kali the essence is the Name of Hari alone;
take that Name and go to the eternal abode.

**232.** Always say "Hari bol!" with thrilling joy;
why make such a commotion in the bazaar of Kali?

**233.** What difference is there between a mansion and a hut?
Go and ask the learned, brothers.

**234.** As a king sleeps on a bedstead in his palace,
so the poor man sleeps in his hut.

**235.** The king drinks water from a golden cup,
the hut-dweller drinks from an earthen pot.

**236.** The aim of both is the same — to quench thirst;
the king's golden cup is mere illusion.

**237.** Does the king eat dal of pearls and curry of gems?
Does he cook his rice out of diamonds?

**238.** Drunk with pride, the king cannot see it,
and so he comes and goes again and again."

*[p. 23]*

**239.** Thus Chaitanya Gosai taught,
and great crowds gathered in one place.

**240.** Seated beneath a bilva tree in Kantaka-nagara,
he gave many teachings in a loud voice.

**241.** The words of his holy mouth are the essence of Vedanta;
whoever hears them is freed from worldly existence.

**242.** Thus a day and a night went by.
The next morning the Lord bathed.

**243.** The women wept, pressing the ends of their saris to their eyes,
their tears pouring down in torrents.

**244.** One said: "May every misfortune that threatens such beauty fall on me![^02-18]
How will his mother stay alive?

**245.** Here the pride of millions of Cupids is humbled;
never have I seen such beautiful hair.

**246.** How lovely is his spotless chin!
His lotus eyes outshine the blue lotus.

**247.** Never have I seen such a wondrous form.
How will Nimai bear the kaupina and the staff?

**248.** Keshava Thakura[^02-19] must be made of stone —
how can he shave off his hair? How cruel!

**249.** Ah, how beautiful is the garland of forest flowers at his throat!
The radiance of his face has lit up every direction."

*[p. 24]*

**250.** So the women said many things.
Meanwhile the Lord quietly called me.

**251.** The Lord said, "Quickly bring all the things that are needed;
I shall shave my head to take sannyasa.

**252.** I will no longer stay at home in bondage;
at home there is the torment of hell at every turn."

**253.** Hearing this, pure-hearted Gadadhara,
the Avadhuta Nityananda and Chandrashekhara

**254.** brought all the requisites fit for sannyasa
and filled the sannyasi's store.

**255.** They called a barber named Deva,
and the barber sat down beneath the bilva tree.

**256.** Then Chaitanya Gosai said to the barber,
"Shave me, Deva — I am going to Vraja."

**257.** Receiving Bharati's order, the barber
sat down close by to shave him.

**258.** When at last the barber put the razor to his hair,
the women all cried out at once.

**259.** The women said, "Barber, do not do this!
Do not shave off such locks of hair!"

**260.** So saying, the women burst into tears,
and then Deva began to shave.

*[p. 25]*

**261.** Thousands upon thousands of people
came flocking to Kantaka-nagara to see the sannyasa.

**262.** At the close of day, his head shaved,
Nimai put on the dress of a sannyasi.

**263.** Staff and water-pot in hand, he put on the kaupina
and covered it with saffron cloth.

**264.** Gosai stood before Bharati,
lighting up every quarter with his beauty — I would give my life for him!

**265.** The Avadhuta, Gadadhara and Gangadasa
one by one stood beside the sannyasi.

**266.** Seeing the Lord's wondrous form, Bharati
bowed inwardly at his lotus feet.

**267.** Inwardly he said, "Gosai, you are God himself;
the whole universe, moving and unmoving, is under you.

**268.** To teach the people you have put on the kaupina;
to show the path of bhakti you have made yourself subject to the lowly."

**269.** In the afternoon the Lord became a sannyasi,
and the women raised the ulu-cry.[^02-20]

**270.** Creepers, leaves, branches and trees floated in love;
beasts, birds and insects seemed to break into dance.

**271.** Hundreds of thousands showered flowers,
and Kantaka-nagara became the Nandana garden.[^02-21]

*[p. 26]*

**272.** Bharati gave him the name Sri Krishna Chaitanya.
Hundreds of thousands came and went.

**273.** The respectable married women, filling their palms,
showered puffed rice on the Lord's head.

**274.** The cry of "Hari!" rose, piercing the sky,
and all rolled on the ground, steeped in devotion.

**275.** The Name, bursting through the heavens, wandered the skies,
and all the gods listened, drunk with joy.

**276.** That night my Lord stayed awake
and passed the night dancing with the Name of Hari.

**277.** In the morning the Lord said to Shekhara:
"All of you go home to Nadia.

**278.** Go with Brahmananda to my mother
and tell her that Nimai has taken sannyasa.

**279.** If my mother weeps,
comfort her with words of reassurance."

**280.** Then, with Nityananda and Gadadhara,
he took Bharati and set out in high spirits.

**281.** I followed behind, carrying the wooden sandals.
Chaitanya Gosai was drunk with the wine of the Name.

**282.** Hundreds of thousands followed the Lord,
and many pandits went along to see him.

*[p. 27]*

**283.** Rudradeva, Ramaratna, Jagai Pandita,
Gangadasa, Shambhuchandra, famed in the world,

**284.** Ishana, Shankara, Balarama, Gadadhara,
Chanda-Chandeshvara, crest-jewel of pandits,

**285.** Kashishvara, Nyayaratna and Siddheshvara,
Panchanana the Vedantist and Ratnakara —

**286.** all these great pandits went with him,
while Sri Krishna Chaitanya, drunk with love, went on in delight.

**287.** Absorbed in dance, the Lord ran ahead —
now running, now leaping, now falling to the ground.

**288.** Tears streamed from his eyes,
and Bharati Gosai wept, tasting that love.

**289.** Then, in ecstasy, he went eastwards
and hurried, drunk with feeling, to the Acharya's house.[^02-22]

**290.** He stayed some time in the Acharya's house,
and meanwhile Mother Shachi came there too.

**291.** Sri Krishna Chaitanya Prabhu bowed
at his mother's feet and spoke to her tenderly.

**292.** After a few words, cutting through the bonds of affection,
he set out southwards, leaving everyone.

**293.** Ishana, Pratapa, Gangadasa, Gadadhara
and Baneshvara went along with the sannyasi.

*[p. 28]*

**294.** When we all reached Burdwan,
I began to wonder what fate had in store for me.

**295.** Looking at me, the Lord began to speak,
as if streams of nectar were flowing out.

**296.** Clapping me on the back, the Lord said,
"Come, Govinda, let us go to your house."

**297.** Hearing this, I started,
and the Lord, laughing, walked on with a swaying gait.

**298.** "When the Lord took sannyasa, I put on the kaupina,
gave up pride and became utterly humble.

**299.** I have no more desire to live a household life;
I will go with the Lord, dancing."

**300.** As we walked along, I folded my hands
and said a few words in reply:

**301.** "I will not go back to Kanchananagar, Lord;
I have given up vile household life like excrement."

**302.** While I was saying this, my wife,
having somehow heard of it, came there in haste.

**303.** Tears were streaming from her eyes,
and she threw herself down at my feet.

**304.** With tears she began to say:
"Come back home, I will go with you.

*[p. 29]*

**305.** Over a trifle you have abandoned the household —
tell me, what provision have you made for your maidservant?

**306.** To whose door shall I go, where shall I beg?
Who will give me alms out of kindness?

**307.** What is in store for me? At whose door
shall I go begging for my belly's sake?"

**308.** Hearing her words, I hung my head
and inwardly kept saying, "Hari, Hari."

**309.** Remembering Hari cuts all bonds;
that is why I fixed my mind on Hari's feet.

**310.** Then merciful Chaitanya, seeing this,
began to speak to her sweet words.

**311.** Hearing the Lord's words, the unhappy woman
began to wet the earth with her tears.

**312.** Distraught with weeping, she looked about her on all sides,
while the Lord tried to make her understand, speaking of the highest truth.

**313.** Hearing those words of the Lord so suddenly,
she covered her eyes with the end of her sari and wept.

**314.** Her weeping moved the Lord to compassion,
and at once he turned to me and said:

**315.** "Govinda, you stay at home;
I will go to Puri with another servant."

*[p. 30]*

**316.** At these words tears poured from my eyes,
and at once I fell, distraught, clasping his feet.

**317.** I washed his two feet with my tears,
and at once the Lord turned and went on.

**318.** Then my neighbours gathered together
and began to talk to me, trying to win me over.

**319.** But I began to speak of the poison of worldly life
and to expound the hidden nature of woman:

**320.** "Listen, brothers, to what I say of woman:
the woman and her lover are one and the same life.

**321.** If now you all turn your gaze to the portion of the Self,
you will see that woman and lover are all one.

**322.** Those who, thinking it sweeter than nectar,
drink a woman's spittle with their eyes closed,

**323.** forgetting eternal bliss and delighting in that —
shame on such a wretch; his birth is wasted.

**324.** My Gauranga is the complete Brahman, the eternal;
shall I abandon his company and take up household life?"

**325.** Saying this, we crossed the Damodar
and went on to the house of Kashi Mitra.[^02-23]

**326.** Kashi Mitra is a virtuous man,
and the Lord went and stayed in his house.

*[p. 31]*

**327.** For the offering Mitra gave good rice,
and the Lord, seeing the rice, praised it.

**328.** The Lord said, "This rice is very fine.
By what name do people call it?"

**329.** Mitra said, "Its name is Jagannatha-bhoga;
when it is offered, every wish is fulfilled."

**330.** Hearing "Jagannatha-bhoga", the Lord started,
and at once streams of love began to flow.

**331.** Weeping, he said, "Ah, Jagannatha!
Quickly draw me to you!"

**332.** Having cooked greens, dal and various dishes,
the Lord, rejoicing, put them all together.

**333.** The smell of the betho greens[^02-24] filled the air,
and before the offering was even made my mind was restless.

**334.** The Lord said, "Quickly bring tulasi;
we will offer the bhoga and I will give you prasada to your heart's content.

**335.** You are very hungry, my child;
that is why you keep looking this way and that."

**336.** I was deeply ashamed at the Lord's words
and stood there with my head hung down.

**337.** He made the offering and distributed the prasada,
and my heart rejoiced at the gravy of the shukta.

*[p. 32]*

**338.** I happily ate half a fried bitter gourd,
lifting big mouthfuls into my mouth.

**339.** Sour chutney[^02-25] with molasses — like nectar;
how much I ate, my face beaming with joy!

**340.** In the afternoon Gora-chand left Mitra's house
and hastened southwards — a snare of love.

**341.** In due course we reached the village of Hajipur,
and there the Lord intoxicated everyone with the Name of Hari.

**342.** Outside the village is a huge banyan tree,
and there we slowly settled down.

**343.** In the evening the Lord began sankirtana,
and the Name rose up into the sky, piercing the heavens.

**344.** The Lord began to dance and intoxicated the whole countryside —
where were his kaupina and waist-string? His dress in disarray.

**345.** Now he falls crashing to the ground,
now, foaming at the mouth, rolls this way and that.

**346.** Hundreds of people gathered there,
and all became intoxicated with the sankirtana of the Name.

**347.** All the men and women together grew drunk with the Name.
Blessed is the power of the Name — I would give my life for it!

**348.** Boys and girls, old men, young men and young women
clapped their hands and danced with devotion.

*[p. 33]*

**349.** Half the night passed in this way,
and then Hari cooked the rice received as alms.

**350.** A village devotee brought ghee,
and with the ghee my Lord fried bitter gourd.

**351.** Shukta with neem, ghee and fried bitter gourd —
my king of Nadia offered them as bhoga.

**352.** Gora-Hari took only a little prasada,
and then I sat down with a leaf-plate.

**353.** I ate the prasada puffing and panting,
*[the second line of the couplet is missing in the edition]*

**354.** and when my belly swelled up,
I went and took refuge at the Lord's feet.

**355.** Then the Lord passed his hand over my belly,
and at once it went back to normal.

**356.** Then I raised the cry "Hari, Hari!",
and the devotees started up in surprise.

**357.** Next morning beautiful Gauranga rose,
called the devotees and spoke with them at length.

**358.** Having reasoned with the devotees, he took his leave
and set off quickly with his companions.

**359.** When he drew near Medinipur,
people heard the news and came running.

*[p. 34]*

**360.** Among them a rich man came up
and was struck dumb on seeing the Lord's form.

**361.** His name was Keshava Samanta, a very wealthy man.
With much guile the rich man talked of many things.

**362.** Now he would say with a laugh, "O best of sannyasis,
take some money, as much as your heart desires.

**363.** Throw off the kaupina and put on proper clothes —
why should a young man take sannyasa?

**364.** Enjoy yourself, yogi, by serving your senses;
why die a slave to renunciation?"

**365.** Hearing the rich man's words, the Lord smiled slightly
and patiently set about teaching him.

**366.** The Lord said, "Money, cowries, gold and emeralds
are all transformations of clay, as the shastras say.

**367.** All that is a transformation of clay will in time become clay.
Why then do you all burst with pride?

**368.** Do not set your feet in the snare of the Lord's maya,
or you will die again and again.

**369.** Whoever wants to die again and again,
the bonds of maya will never let him go.

**370.** Give up everything and worship Him with devotion;
then supreme bliss will arise in your heart.

*[p. 35]*

**371.** You wander about saying 'mine, mine',
not knowing you have already entered the jaws of Time.

**372.** When it grinds you between its teeth,
where will your beautiful women be then?

**373.** Where will your gold, your silver and your grain be?
Where will your thickened milk, cream and curd-cheese be?

**374.** This cherished body will burn to ashes,
and if it does not burn, jackals will eat it.

**375.** Your head will roll in the filth by the leather-worker's door.[^02-26]
Worship Krishna, say 'Krishna' — time is passing in vain.

**376.** What are you proud of? Everything is transient.
The eternal reality is Krishna, the place where one finds peace.

**377.** O best of rich men, listen to my words:
do you eat diamonds, pearls and emeralds?

**378.** A handful of rice is enough to stop hunger;
why then are you proud all the time?"

**379.** Having taught the rich man thus,
the Lord looked at me and said a few words:

**380.** "[Let us go to Narayangarh,][^02-27] let us go;
perhaps we shall find some joy there."

**381.** Saying just this, he rose quickly,
and at once I lifted the sandals onto my shoulder.

*[p. 36]*

**382.** While my Gora walked happily along the road,
in the evening we reached that place.

**383.** In Narayangarh there is a Shiva, Dhaleshvara,
and he hurried to see him.

**384.** He is the village deity of Narayangarh.
The Lord began to weep, his tears flowing in streams.

**385.** Crying aloud, "Hara, Hara!",
the Lord fell crashing to the ground.

**386.** Choked with love, he rolled on the ground,
and his cloth and water-pot fell who knows where.

**387.** The great sattvika ecstasies[^02-28] arose in him,
and in his love blood spurted from his pores.

**388.** His outer cloth and kaupina slipped off who knows where;
how many ascetics came there to see that state!

**389.** Vanaprasthas, brahmacharis, so many renunciants
came there to see the Lord of Nadia.

**390.** Seeing his love and devotion, all were amazed
and fell to the ground, crying, "He is a god!"

**391.** Raising the cry of "Hari!", the Lord began to dance,
and seeing that tremendous state, all shuddered.

**392.** Thus everyone was dancing beneath the tree.
*[the second line of the couplet is missing in the edition]*

*[p. 37]*

**393.** I, a sinner and the lowest of men, kept eyeing the laddus,[^02-29]
and was seized by a craving to eat my fill of them.

**394.** The all-knowing Lord understood me by a hint,
and, making the laddus into prasada, gave them to me to eat.

**395.** I ate five laddus and filled my belly,
and a brahmana brought me cool water.

**396.** In time the villagers heard the news
and one by one gathered at that place.

**397.** The Lord made the offering and distributed the prasada,
and all of them together received it there.

**398.** Seeing their devotion to the prasada, I thought hard:
I, the glutton, had filled my belly before everyone else.

**399.** Thinking this and repenting inwardly,
to destroy my sin I clasped the Lord's feet.

**400.** He reasoned with me in many words and placed his foot on my head,
and at once the confusion left my mind.

**401.** Then in ecstasy he began to dance,
and all grew drunk on the wine of the nectar of Hari.

**402.** Some danced, some rolled on the ground,
some ran towards the trees crying "Krishna!"

**403.** Gradually a great crowd gathered at that place.
*[the second line of the couplet is missing in the edition]*

*[p. 38]*

**404.** Hearing of the new sannyasi,
everyone came to that place one after another.

**405.** Vireshvara Sena and Bhavani Shankara
came before the Lord with many people.

**406.** With palanquins, elephants, horses and many carriages
they came into the Lord's presence.

**407.** Bhavani Shankara is a very rich man;
he came with hundreds of people.

**408.** Kettle-drums on camels' backs, colourful banners,
four silver howdahs going on ahead.

**409.** All of them worms of worldliness, drunk with pride;
seeing them, the Lord's heart filled with compassion.

**410.** Seeing their condition, merciful Chaitanya
blessed them by giving them devotion.

**411.** Teaching devotion, the Lord intoxicated them all,
and more than a hundred thousand listened, still as puppets.

**412.** With a straw in his teeth and folded hands, the Lord said,
"All of you, listen to my humble words."

**413.** The Lord said, "Listen, all you wealthy gentlemen:
this world is like a juggler's trick.

**414.** When, overcome by sleep, you mount a throne,
then the thought 'I am king' arises in your mind.

*[p. 39]*

**415.** Hundreds of ministers and friends deliberate,
hundreds of thousands of subjects come with gifts.

**416.** But you never reflect on what all this is.
Fool, understand: it is the shadow of a reflection.

**417.** The inert world is a reflection of the truth of Krishna,
and dream is a reflection of that — so say the Vedas.

**418.** Think it over: both of them are dreams;
the only difference is sleeping or waking.

**419.** All the king's kingdom is a waking dream.
Consider what is true and what is false by the word of the Vedas.

**420.** Gold, silver, gems and pearls are transformations of clay;
the precious thing is Krishna — this is the essence.

**421.** The eternal reality is Bhagavan, so say the Vedas,
and all else that you see is false.

**422.** One who stays submerged under water —
how can he see the things on dry land?

**423.** If you lift him out of the water onto the bank,
then he will see the things of the land close by.

**424.** So it is with one who is sunk in worldliness —
how can he see Radha and Krishna?

**425.** He over whose eyes the blinkers of maya are tied
is always blind, like the oil-mill bullock.

*[p. 40]*

**426.** Who knows what lies inside a mountain cave?
How can one know its truth from outside?

**427.** Just so the subtle nature of the inert world —
who can perceive it by gross means?

**428.** The endless universes are the Lord's form;
he alone understands this who gives up the path of ritual works.
